## E.7 Monitoring, reporting and verification arrangements (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

### A. U.N. Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) monitoring activities
A UN Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) Monitoring Officer will be employed to conduct and coordinate monitoring and evaluation (M&E) activities for the project. The Monitoring Officer will work closely with the Project Manager and other relevant staff to ensure that all M&E activities are performed according to the project indicators. Section 63-61 include the design of the project, reporting the project's performance, the UNFCCC and related monitoring activities. The Monitoring Officer will be responsible for developing a comprehensive M&E plan, including regular performance reports, and ensuring that all project outputs and outcomes are met. The Monitoring Officer will also be responsible for developing indicators to measure progress against the project's objectives and reporting on the project's performance.

### B. Internal Evaluation
The UNFCCC Task Manager will increase the prospects of being in external industries. This will be undertaken to carry out the same evaluation, who will provide an assessment of the project's performance. The internal evaluation will be conducted by a team of experts from the relevant departments and will be responsible for evaluating the project's progress against the project indicators. The UNFCCC Task Manager will also be responsible for developing indicators to measure progress against the project's objectives.

### C. Final Evaluation
The final evaluation will be conducted by the UNFCCC Evaluation Office in concert with the final evaluation of the end of project implementation. The Evaluation Office (EO) will be responsible for overseeing the final evaluation and the end of project implementation, which is a summative evaluation, and will provide an assessment of the project's overall performance. The final evaluation will assess the project's achievements, resources, effectiveness, outcomes, sustainability, and will shed light on lessons learned from the project. The final evaluation will also be used to identify areas for improvement and to develop recommendations for future projects. The final evaluation will be conducted by a team of experts who will provide an assessment of the project's performance.

### D. E.3.2.9.4 Annual progress reports
Annual progress reports will be prepared and submitted to the UNFCCC Secretariat and other relevant stakeholders.

## V.3.1: PAGE 81

The inherent complexity of projects that have evolved in the midst of the pandemic requires that they be subject to a more rigorous and comprehensive monitoring and evaluation plan than has been designed for the past, which will provide a basis for learning and adaptation. This is particularly important as projects are following their implementation plans during the proposed project’s implementation period. This can be supported by regular monitoring and evaluation.

### Selected Risk Factor 4

**Category:** Moderate

**Probability:** Likely

**Impact:** Moderate

**Description:**

Limited participation by women and other marginalized groups in project development and decision making could lead to a lack of trust in the process. This can result in lower levels of community engagement and support for similar projects. More women should be involved in planning activities, as well as in implementing and monitoring the project.

**Mitigation Measures:**

- Ensure that women are included in all stages of the project.
- Provide training on gender equality to all stakeholders.
- Develop a gender-responsive budgeting process.
- Establish a gender equality committee within the project team.
- Monitor and evaluate the impact of gender equality initiatives throughout the project lifecycle.

### Selected Risk Factor 5

**Category:** Moderate

**Probability:** Likely

**Impact:** Moderate

**Description:**

Changes in agricultural labor laws are as likely to be beneficial as they are to be detrimental. The impact will depend on how these changes affect the livelihoods of farmers and other project participants.

**Mitigation Measures:**

- Conduct a thorough analysis of the potential impacts of any new labor laws.
- Develop contingency plans to address any negative impacts that may arise.
- Provide training and support to help farmers adapt to any changes in labor laws.
- Monitor the impact of any changes in labor laws on the project and adjust implementation strategies accordingly.

### Selected Risk Factor 6

**Category:** Low

**Probability:** Likely

**Impact:** Moderate

**Description:**

Changes in agricultural labor laws are as likely to be beneficial as they are to be detrimental. The impact will depend on how these changes affect the livelihoods of farmers and other project participants.

**Mitigation Measures:**

- Conduct a thorough analysis of the potential impacts of any new labor laws.
- Develop contingency plans to address any negative impacts that may arise.
- Provide training and support to help farmers adapt to any changes in labor laws.
- Monitor the impact of any changes in labor laws on the project and adjust implementation strategies accordingly.

### Selected Risk Factor 7

**Category:** Low

**Probability:** Likely

**Impact:** Moderate

**Description:**

Global trends such as the COVID-19 pandemic can have significant impacts on the agricultural sector. These trends can affect the availability of inputs, labor, and markets for project outputs.

**Mitigation Measures:**

- Monitor global trends that may impact the project.
- Develop contingency plans to address any disruptions caused by global trends.
- Diversify project activities to reduce dependence on a single market or input.
- Establish partnerships with other organizations to share risks and resources.

### C.1. Environmental and Social Risk Assessment (yes, 70% work, approximately 1.5 pages)

#### Project Preparation

The project will be prepared in such a way that it has been included to conform to the GCP Environmental and Social Safeguards. The proposed project is classified as Category I (Immunized), based on the ESMF.

##### S9.1. Environmental and Social Management Action Result Monitoring

The inclusion of Project Specific Indicators as a primary component for following the environmental consequences of the project is essential. These indicators will be included in the ESMP and will be used to monitor the performance of the project. The GCP will include for these indicators, including those that apply to proper management of the environment, social issues, and other relevant issues. There is also a need to include relevant and other well-proven procedures for monitoring and evaluation of environmental and social safeguards that are consistent with best practices.

The ESMP will be prepared in accordance with the GCP Environmental and Social Safeguard Policy Statement (ESMP). The ESMP will be reviewed by the GCP Environmental and Social Safeguard Team. The ESMP will be updated to include any new information or changes in the project that may affect the environmental and social safeguards.

##### Stakeholder Consultation

The project is prepared in accordance with the ESMF, which contains an analysis of the general impact of implementation and the potential risks associated with it. A detailed risk assessment is required for a Category I (Immunized) project.

##### Project Preparation Meeting Mechanism

A meeting will be held to discuss the project and its preparation process. The GCP will be represented by the Environmental and Social Safeguard Team, which includes a full list of participants from the ESMF. These discussions are expected to continue during the implementation phase of the project. The GCP will also include a list of participants who will be involved in the project.

##### Accreditation

The accreditation process will be used as a condition for ESMF and ongoing activities. The GCP will use this process to ensure that the project is implemented according to the ESMF. The GCP will also use this process to ensure that the project is implemented according to the ESMF.

##### Project Preparation Meeting Mechanism

The GCP will be represented by the Environmental and Social Safeguard Team, which includes a full list of participants from the ESMF. These discussions are expected to continue during the implementation phase of the project. The GCP will also include a list of participants who will be involved in the project.

### Steps

1. **Preparation of comprehensive environmental and social risk assessment.**
2. **Review of the ESMP by the Environmental and Social Safeguard Team.**
3. **Conduct of an initial stakeholder consultation meeting.**
4. **Implementation and revision of comprehensive environmental and social safeguards.**

# GENDER ASSESSMENT AND ACTION PLAN (GAAP)

## 1.00 Introduction

A Gender Assessment and Action Plan (GAAP) for the project provides an overview of gender-disaggregated data and analysis to ensure that the project is designed to meet its objectives, including gender equality and social inclusion (GESI). The GAAP will be developed in line with the World Bank's GESI operational policy 3.01 and the Gender Equality Strategy (GES) adopted by the Board of Directors in October 2018.

### 1.10 Purpose

The purpose of this document is to:

- Provide an overview of gender-disaggregated data and analysis.
- Identify key gender issues and opportunities for addressing them.
- Develop a plan for integrating GESI into project activities.

## 2.00 Gender Analysis

A gender analysis involves examining the different ways in which women, men, girls, and boys experience development differently. This includes analyzing the differential impacts of policies, programs, and projects on women and men, as well as identifying opportunities to promote gender equality.

### 2.10 Methodology

The methodology for this GAAP will include:

- A review of secondary data sources.
- Consultations with stakeholders.
- Analysis of project activities and outcomes.

## 3.00 Action Plan

Based on the findings of the gender analysis, a set of actions will be developed to address key gender issues identified in the project. The action plan will include specific objectives, indicators, and targets for each activity.

### 3.10 Objectives

The objectives of the GAAP will be aligned with the project's overall goals and may include:

- Increasing women's participation in project activities.
- Improving access to services for marginalized groups.
- Promoting gender equality in project decision-making processes.

## 4.00 Implementation

The implementation of the GAAP will involve:

- Integrating GESI into project design, implementation, and monitoring.
- Regularly reviewing progress towards objectives.
- Adjusting activities as needed based on feedback and results.

### 4.10 Monitoring and Evaluation

Monitoring and evaluation (M&E) will be an integral part of the GAAP. It will include:

- Developing indicators to track progress towards objectives.
- Collecting data regularly to assess performance.
- Reporting on findings and making recommendations for improvement.

# The Green Climate Fund (GCF)

## GCF Environment programme (GEP) targeting strengthening the resilience of ecosystems and populations to natural hazards in Morocco

Dear Madam/Sir,

We call to the governments which strengthen the resilience of ecosystems and populations in Morocco through the implementation of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Environment Programme (GEP) to be held on September 2023.

The Government of Morocco has been selected as a National Designated Authority (NDA) for the GCF. The NDA is responsible for the preparation and inclusion of the funding proposal in the GEP.

### Purpose

The purpose of this letter is to invite the government to be included in the funding proposal.

### Eligibility Criteria

Projects are included in the funding proposal if they comply with the following criteria:

- Projects must be implemented by national governments.
- Projects must be implemented by local authorities, the private sector or civil society organizations.
- Projects must be implemented at the national level and/or local level.
- Projects must be implemented for a period of 5 years.

We also encourage projects that are implemented in partnership with other countries or actors to be included in the funding proposal.

### Application Process

Applications will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Sincerely,

Submitted by: [Signature]

Date: [Date]

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP266

## Proposal name
Strengthening the readiness of communities and populations in Mauritius to adapt to climate change impacts through improved early warning systems.

## Accepted entity
United Nations Environmental Programme (UNEP)

## Country
Mauritius

## Project size
Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### Strengths
- **Economic benefit adaptation approach**: The project aims to enhance the resilience of coastal communities by utilizing early warning systems.
- **Environmental benefits**: The project will reduce the risk of flooding and improve water quality, benefiting 120 kilometers of protected coastal areas.
- **Social benefits**: The project will actively involve communities in planning and implementation, ensuring local ownership and sustainability.

### Points of caution
- **Funding regions where the project activities are implemented**: The funding region is limited to specific areas within Mauritius, which could lead to uneven development between sites.
- **Small-scale implementation**: The project’s small scale may limit its ability to demonstrate broader applicability.
- **Environmental benefits**: The project will enhance community resilience in Mauritius, which could be replicated and scaled up. It aligns with the full-time Climate Change Adaptation Unit for multiple countries under the United Nations Environment Programme Task Manager.

### Recommendations
The project will establish a Small Grants Programme to support local communities in developing and implementing small-scale projects.
- **Economic benefits**: The project aims to provide sustainable employment opportunities based on community needs, providing foundational support to local economies.
- **Environmental benefits**: The project will help reduce the risk of climate change impacts.

The board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the following conditions:
1. The project should be implemented in accordance with the approved budget and work plan.
2. The project should be subject to the conditions set out in annex 1 to document the project’s implementation.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background


---
**Page 77**
---

# Climate Change

Climate change is profoundly impacting Maurtitian communities, particularly those living on the southern atolls and islands, which are vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. The adverse effects include sea-level rise, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as cyclones, droughts, and floods. These changes have significant implications for the environment, economy, and social fabric of the country.

## Climate-Induced Challenges

The profound impact focuses on addressing these climate-induced challenges by enhancing resilience and adaptive capacity through sustainable development initiatives. Targeted interventions will be implemented to strengthen community-based disaster risk management systems, improve early warning systems, and promote climate-smart agriculture. These efforts aim to enhance the country's overall preparedness for future climate-related events.

## Expected Benefits

The project is expected to directly benefit 3,228 people (60% women and 40% men), including 1,597 women and 1,631 men, totaling 3,228 beneficiaries. The total project funding amounts to USD 33,660,315, of which a 67% grant from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) is being used. The project will be implemented under the relevant national policies and strategies.

# Component-by-component analysis

## Component 1: Governance and Capacity Building

Objective: Strengthened coordination and capacity of government institutions to implement climate change adaptation measures.

### Output 1.1: Improved governance for adaptation

Output 1.1 will focus on enhancing the ability of the Government of Maurtitius to coordinate and mainstream climate change adaptation into national policies and strategies. This component aims to strengthen the capacity of regional and local governments to support climate-related initiatives, enhance coordination among stakeholders, and promote effective partnerships in vulnerable areas.

### Output 1.2: Enhanced capacity for climate change adaptation

Output 1.2 will focus on enhancing the capacity of government institutions to implement climate change adaptation measures. This includes providing training and technical assistance to build the skills and knowledge needed to effectively manage climate-related risks. The component will also support the development of tools and resources to enhance the resilience of communities and ecosystems.

## Additional Outputs

Additional outputs will include the creation and dissemination of knowledge products that showcase successful practices and policy briefs. These products will aid in enhancing the overall understanding of climate change adaptation measures and promoting best practices across the region.


---
**Page 78**
---

# Informing Decision Making and the Use of Incremental EIA Practice

Informed decision making and the use of incremental EIA practice enhances the resilience of recipients and communities. The integration of dynamic change considerations within the EIA process can enhance the capacity to adapt to a range of potential future scenarios, clustering long-term sustainability beyond the project's lifetime.

## Enhancing Resilience through Incremental EIA Practice

Incremental EIA practice enables decision makers to establish baseline conditions and monitor changes over time. This allows for the establishment of baselines and establishes a process that can be adapted to changing circumstances. The use of incremental EIA practice can enhance the capacity to adapt to changing circumstances, enabling communities to respond to new challenges with greater resilience.

### Component 1: Strengthening Incremental Resilience and Climate-Resilient Livelihoods

Outcome 2: Communities in their area of action build upon the benefits derived from Mountain Ecosystem Services (MES) and enhance their adaptive capacity through improved livelihoods, food security, and water security. This outcome will be achieved by:

1. One of the core activities under this component is establishing a 2-3 year long-term evaluation framework that will enable communities to monitor changes in their environment and adapt accordingly. This framework will include indicators of environmental change, such as changes in water availability, soil quality, and biodiversity.
2. In addition to long-term evaluation, the component focuses on improving access for communities to climate-resilient livelihoods through improved agricultural practices, sustainable forest management, and enhanced water security. This includes activities that improve community resilience by enhancing local natural resource management and promoting sustainable use of resources.
3. The component will also support climate-resilient agriculture livelihoods by providing training and technical assistance to farmers on climate-smart agricultural practices. This will include activities such as the development of climate-resilient crops, improved irrigation systems, and enhanced soil fertility.

Outcome 8: Communities in their area of action build upon the benefits derived from Mountain Ecosystem Services (MES) and enhance their adaptive capacity through improved livelihoods, food security, and water security. This outcome will be achieved by:

1. One of the core activities under this component is establishing a 2-3 year long-term evaluation framework that will enable communities to monitor changes in their environment and adapt accordingly. This framework will include indicators of environmental change, such as changes in water availability, soil quality, and biodiversity.
2. In addition to long-term evaluation, the component focuses on improving access for communities to climate-resilient livelihoods through improved agricultural practices, sustainable forest management, and enhanced water security. This includes activities that improve community resilience by enhancing local natural resource management and promoting sustainable use of resources.
3. The component will also support climate-resilient agriculture livelihoods by providing training and technical assistance to farmers on climate-smart agricultural practices. This will include activities such as the development of climate-resilient crops, improved irrigation systems, and enhanced soil fertility.

# Long-term economic viability

Long-term economic viability is supported by private economic indicators. The economic analysis estimates that net present value (NPV) of USD 153.5 million exceeds a 20-year payback period. The internal rate of return (IRR) is 48%, and the benefit-cost ratio (BCR) is 29 for the project. The analysis uses a 10% real discount rate, a 60-year project life, and a non-worker scenario that includes the strong resilience to natural hazards. The economic analysis also considers the water economy-wide benefits and the financial viability of the project.

## Financial analysis

The financial analysis presents beneficial levels such as flows and future costs under "what-if" scenarios. The financial analysis is based on 12-year cost-benefit (CB) scenarios, including the financial IRR from 12 years up to 25 years. Beneficial net present value (NPV) is USD 384 million. The analysis shows that most beneficial value accrues via subsistence consumption, fishing, and tourism. The financial analysis also considers the benefits of reduced flood risk, improved water quality, and increased agricultural productivity. Land values increase by 10% in the project area. The financial analysis is modeled after the financial analysis. Further development of capacity-specific financial models for the project will be required to fully understand the financial viability and sustainability of these subprojects from the financial analysis.

Adaptation costs are directly beneficial to estimated at USD 362.8, and (USD) 134.9 per person per year. The adaptation cost is estimated at USD 50 million over a 20-year period. When the project is implemented, the adaptation cost will be reduced by 70%. The adaptation cost is estimated to be USD 156 million over a 20-year period. **Conditionalities**

Conditionalities are sometimes employed to ensure value for money and to mitigate occupational health and safety risks in project employees and implementing entities. These may include the following: (1) adherence to labor laws, including minimum wage requirements and fair compensation; (2) compliance with international poor labor and working conditions rules; (3) physical activities or civil works that are safe and do not expose workers to unacceptable levels of risk; (4) acceptable test practices; (5) regular audits and inspections by the implementing agency; (6) adherence to environmental standards; and (7) compliance monitoring by the employees themselves, their line management, and the PMU.

**Community Engagement**

Community engagement is an important component of any project. It is essential that community members are involved in the planning and implementation of interventions. This will ensure that the project is responsive to local needs and priorities and that it is implemented effectively. Community engagement should be ongoing throughout the life cycle of the project, from planning through to implementation and evaluation.

**Informed Consent**

Informed consent is an important component of any research or intervention involving human subjects. It is essential that participants are fully informed about the nature of the study or intervention, its potential risks and benefits, and their rights as participants. Informed consent should be obtained from all participants before they are enrolled in the study or intervention.

**Institutional Review Boards**

Institutional review boards (IRBs) are committees that review research protocols to ensure that they meet ethical standards and protect the rights of human subjects. IRBs are typically composed of scientists, ethicists, and community members who review research proposals and provide recommendations to the principal investigator.

**Good Clinical Practice**

Good clinical practice (GCP) is an international standard for the design, conduct, performance, monitoring, analysis, recording, reporting, and storage of data in clinical trials. GCP ensures that trials are conducted ethically and according to good scientific principles.

**Informed Consent Process**

The informed consent process involves providing potential participants with information about a study or intervention, including its purpose, risks, benefits, and alternatives. The process also includes obtaining the participant's voluntary agreement to participate in the study or intervention.

# A.3 Risks

## A.3.1 Overall project assessment (medium risk)
- The contractual assessment overall programme risk is medium.

## A.3.2 Accreditation entity/accepting money capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)
- UNIP will serve as the accreditation entity for this project. UNIP has experience in executing similar projects and has a good track record of working with a Board of 10-15 members to provide oversight, including financial management and procurement systems in place. However, the AI team has not always been able to execute on time due to lack of resources and technical expertise. The project will be executed by the AI team with support from the UNIP Task Force.
- The AI team has a good track record of executing projects on time and within budget.

## A.3.3 Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)
- Implementation challenges: The four largest regions where the project will be implemented have been identified as having the highest number of household deliveries of each other. The distance between these regions may present logistical challenges in terms of coordination and communication. This could impact the project budget and timeline.
- Lack of buy-in: There is a lack of endorsement of the project amongst the regions, as well as potential resistance to working with the project team. This could lead to delays or even derailment of the project. The project team will need to work closely with the regions to ensure their support and engagement in the project.
- Lack of resources: There is a lack of resources available to execute the project effectively, including funding and technical expertise. This could impact the project's ability to achieve its objectives.

## A.3.4 Compliance risk (medium risk)
- The business landscape/partnership financing (M/F) risks associated with certain regions are medium due to the lack of a clear regulatory framework for microfinance institutions, as well as potential challenges in obtaining financing from traditional sources.
- The project will be implemented by a consortium of local government and financial management experts and is a risk assessment of the project's compliance with relevant regulations. The project team will need to ensure that the project complies with all relevant laws and regulations and that it is implemented in accordance with best practices.

# 4.4 Fiduciary

The project will be implemented by the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) as the accredited entity (AE) in collaboration with the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MENR). UNEP will ensure overall responsibility for the project implementation, fiduciary compliance, financial management, procurement, and environmental safeguards. The AE will also ensure that all actions are managed in accordance with agreed plans. A comprehensive risk assessment will be conducted by the AE to identify potential risks and develop mitigation measures.

Overall direction will be provided by a Project Steering Committee (PSC) comprised of a representative from UNEP, a representative from MENR, and a representative from the Ministry of Finance. Financial management will be governed by the financial regulations and rules of UNEP. UNEP will ensure that all procurement is conducted in accordance with UNEP's procurement policies and procedures, including the use of competitive bidding processes where appropriate. All financial management activities will be overseen by a Project Board comprised of representatives from UNEP, MENR, and the Ministry of Finance.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP266

## Proposed name
Strengthening the resilience of communities and ecosystems in four regional sites in northern Mauritania

## Accredited entity
United Nations Environmental Programme (UNEP)

## Country
Mauritania

## L. Assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Overview

1. This funding proposal for a small-sized adaptation project is environmental and social development in nature.
2. The proposed project is located in northern Mauritania, which is part of the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) as the proposed project’s accredited entity (AE). The total cost of the funding proposal is USD 3.54 million, of which USD 3.07 million is a grant from the Least Developed Countries Fund (LDCF), and USD 0.47 million is co-financing from the Government of Mauritania. The project implementation period is six years, while benefits are indicated over a duration of 20 years.
3. The proposed project’s objective is to enhance the resilience of communities and ecosystems in four regional sites in northern Mauritania by restoring degraded land and promoting sustainable land management practices. The project will focus on four regional sites (one per region) that include: (i) Trarza, (ii) Brakna, (iii) Assaba, and (iv) Hodh El Gharbi (southern part of the Hodh El Chargui). The project designs to restore degraded land through sustainable land management practices such as integrated water resource management, land rehabilitation, land use planning, and livelihood diversification. Land drought and flood with a particular focus on women’s empowerment.

Observations of historical climate trends show that the mean annual temperature in Madagascar has increased by 0.3°C per decade over the past 50 years. This warming is expected to continue at a rate of about 0.2°C per decade for the next century, with an increase in the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, and cyclones. These changes are likely to have significant impacts on human health, agriculture, water resources, and biodiversity.

# Green Climate Fund

and habitats, with vast amounts covering entire villages. Villages of dams, reservoirs, wells and rivers are nested in the accessibility and mobility of already scarce water resources to the local communities. The impact of climate change is evident in the increasing frequency and intensity of droughts and floods, which are increasingly threatening the lives of people living near water tables. This has led to many problems with drying up of wells and springs, and the consequent loss of livelihoods for farmers and fishermen who depend on these natural resources for their survival.

## Adaptation impacts

The adaptation impacts potential in the project is considerable, as it will improve the resilience of communities to climate change. The project will support the development of water harvesting systems, which will be integrated into the landscape design. This will be achieved through the construction of small-scale water storage facilities and the use of rainwater harvesting techniques. These measures will also help to reduce the vulnerability of water resources, agricultural production and livelihoods to drought and floods.

### Adaptation measures

Adaptation measures will be used to reduce vulnerability to flood and drought stresses. These include:

- **Water harvesting systems**: These systems will collect and store rainwater for use during dry periods.
- **Soil conservation techniques**: These techniques will help to improve soil fertility and water retention, reducing the risk of erosion and flooding.
- **Agricultural practices**: These practices will be adapted to changing climatic conditions, such as the use of drought-resistant crops and improved irrigation systems.

### Benefits

The project is expected to directly benefit 3,275 people (16477 were and 43,52 women) through improved access to water resources. The project will also provide indirect benefits to an additional 8,000 people (4098 men and 3902 women). These benefits include:

- Improved access to clean water for drinking, cooking and sanitation.
- Reduced vulnerability to drought and floods.
- Increased agricultural productivity through improved irrigation systems.

### Risks

Risks to new biological infrastructure, loss of biodiversity, and changes in ecosystem services are potential impacts. The project will aim to mitigate these risks by implementing measures such as:

- **Biodiversity monitoring**: Regular monitoring of biodiversity will be conducted to ensure that the project does not negatively impact local ecosystems.
- **Ecosystem restoration**: Measures will be implemented to restore any damaged ecosystems.

## Benefits to new biological infrastructure

Loss of biodiversity and changes in ecosystem services are potential impacts. The project will aim to mitigate these risks by implementing measures such as:

- **Biodiversity monitoring**: Regular monitoring of biodiversity will be conducted to ensure that the project does not negatively impact local ecosystems.
- **Ecosystem restoration**: Measures will be implemented to restore any damaged ecosystems.

## Risks

Risks to new biological infrastructure, loss of biodiversity, and changes in ecosystem services are potential impacts. The project will aim to mitigate these risks by implementing measures such as:

- **Biodiversity monitoring**: Regular monitoring of biodiversity will be conducted to ensure that the project does not negatively impact local ecosystems.
- **Ecosystem restoration**: Measures will be implemented to restore any damaged ecosystems.

# 1.3 Paradigm shift potential

The paradigm shift has several key features that are essential to the GFE second pillar, aimed at enhancing the knowledge base of ecosystems, and the development of a new generation of knowledge products and services. These include:

- Promoting (i) sustainable innovation; (ii) modeling finances at local and regional levels; and (iii) developing new knowledge products and services that are tailored to the needs of target users.
- The paradigm shift will be applied at a sub-national level in the communities of the four target countries, with the support of the GFE second pillar. The development of these knowledge products will be based on the principle of "knowledge is linked to sustainable livelihoods," and common best practices will be identified and shared among the four target countries.
- Knowledge-sharing and learning: Continuous project study and assessment will be conducted to identify lessons learned, success stories, and best practices that can be used to improve the quality and sustainability of future projects. This will include sharing across various platforms, including social media and webinars, to promote quality and equity, and to ensure that knowledge is shared widely.
- The GFE knowledge platform will also be used as a community repository accessible to all stakeholders, including governments, civil society organizations, and the private sector. It will support and incorporate new knowledge products, enhancing the depth and integration of existing knowledge products. This will help to identify gaps in knowledge and provide opportunities for targeted learning and capacity-building.
- These knowledge products will also be integrated into the country's national adaptation plans and climate change strategies.

## Existing instruments

It is common for every government to put in place a set of policies and regulations that guide the development, implementation, and enforcement of environmental laws. These include:

- The Environmental Management Act (EMA) 1986.
- The National Environment Management Authority (NEMA) Act 1999.

### The EMA approach

The EMA approach that the project takes a bottom-up approach to addressing environmental issues by involving local communities and other stakeholders in the development of environmental policies. This is achieved through the development and implementation of knowledge products, including policy briefs, guidelines, and best practices. These will be developed with the support of other regional government agencies that work on long-term climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts.

### The NEMA approach

The NEMA approach is a top-down approach to addressing environmental issues by involving national governments in the development of environmental policies. This is achieved through the development and implementation of knowledge products, including policy briefs, guidelines, and best practices. These will be developed with the support of other regional government agencies that work on long-term climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts.

# Sustainable development potential

The proposed project is aligned with the United Nations 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development and its 17 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The SDGs most relevant to this project include:

- **SDG 2**: Zero Hunger.
- **SDG 5**: Gender Equality.
- **SDG 6**: Clean Water and Sanitation.
- **SDG 8**: Decent Work and Economic Growth.
- **SDG 9**: Industry, Innovation, and Infrastructure.
- **SDG 11**: Sustainable Cities and Communities.
- **SDG 12**: Responsible Consumption and Production.
- **SDG 13**: Climate Action.
- **SDG 14**: Life Below Water.
- **SDG 15**: Life on Land.

## Environmental co-benefits

Capturing rainwater and allowing infiltration through on-site retention systems will reduce the volume of stormwater runoff. This natural regeneration of wetlands and wet systems in degraded grazing lands, including native vegetation restoration, will benefit biodiversity. These actions will also reduce soil erosion and improve water quality. The project will provide a natural habitat for local wildlife, enhancing biodiversity and promoting ecosystem health.

## Gender and social inclusion

The gender assessment and action plan consider the needs of women and men to ensure that both genders are included in the project's benefits. This includes addressing the specific needs of women and girls who benefit less from traditional infrastructure projects. The project will also address the needs of vulnerable groups, including people with disabilities.

## Addressing gender equality

The proposed project interventions will address gender equality through several activities, such as providing training on sustainable agriculture practices for women's groups. This will empower women to participate in decision-making processes and improve their livelihoods. The project will also support community-driven action plans based on the specific gaps identified within each community.

# The monster's high public and external debt - estimated at 79 per cent of GDP and 48 per cent of GNI respectively - constrains the ability of the Government to increase spending from its national budget in order to address the needs of the poor. The Government is committed to maintaining a primary surplus of 1.5 per cent of GDP, which is expected to result in limited fiscal space for additional spending. Such expenditure is required to enhance the resilience of the population to climate change and natural disasters, improve access to basic services, and boost economic growth. The Government will continue to implement its National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and other initiatives to strengthen the resilience of the population to climate change and natural disasters. The Government's high public debt is expected to result in limited fiscal space for additional spending from the national budget - on both a short- and long-term basis. This high level of external debt also constrains the ability of the Government to increase its capital expenditure, which is required to enhance economic growth.

## The vulnerability of the population of Mauritius

The vulnerability of the population of Mauritius is high because of their exposure to natural hazards such as cyclones, floods, and droughts. In addition, areas that have services and facilities are exposed to coastal erosion and flooding. The population of Mauritius is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts, including sea-level rise, which is expected to affect the population of Mauritius by 2050. The vulnerability of the population of Mauritius is exacerbated by the high levels of poverty in the country. The Government has identified a number of measures that can be taken to reduce the vulnerability of the population of Mauritius to climate change impacts.

## Overall, based on the above, the needs of the residents are rated as high.

# Country ownership

The proposed project has been designed following extensive consultations with the Government and a range of stakeholders, and is aligned with the policies of Mauritius. The value of the AF project is estimated at USD 10 million, which is equivalent to approximately USD 35 per capita. The project will be implemented in close collaboration with the Government and other stakeholders.

## Points and strategies alignment

The proposed project aligns well with the Government's national adaptation strategy, which aims to strengthen resilience among a range of sectors. The project is also aligned with the national adaptation plan (NAP) process, which was launched in 2015. The project directly addresses two of the objectives of the NAP: (i) strengthening adaptive capacity and resilience against climate change impacts; and (ii) strengthening institutional and technical capacities at local, regional, and national levels to implement adaptation measures.

The proposed project is also aligned with the Government's National Adaptation Plan (NAP) process. The project will be implemented in close collaboration with the Government and other stakeholders. The project will be implemented over a period of five years, from 2015 to 2020.

# The Global Fund requesting USD 38.9 billion to grant finance from GEFF to be supported by its loan to financing (USD 2.45) billion from the MDBs and regional authorities.

The GF will continue to support the implementation of the GF/GEFF program in the next funding cycle, focusing on resource-efficient and water resource management. The GF funds both per capita and per unit of production, with a focus on the most vulnerable countries. The GF will continue to support the MDBs and regional authorities in the public goods governance nature of the ecological infrastructure.

## Beyond the direct co-financing projects

The Global Fund is committed to strengthening the proposed project's strategic partnerships to ensure that the project is implemented in line with the objectives set out by the Global Fund. The Global Fund will continue to support the implementation of the project through its loan to financing (USD 2.45) billion from the MDBs and regional authorities.

The Global Fund will be used to support local, regional and national institutions to implement actions to improve resource efficiency and water resource management. The Global Fund will also provide continued investment in ecosystem restoration, climate-smart agriculture, and sustainable development.

## Input potential

We acknowledge IAP’s assessment that the project’s input potential is “medium”. We note that no specific recommendations are made in this regard.

### Paradigm shift potential

We acknowledge IAP’s conclusion that the project’s paradigm shift potential is “moderate” owing to its potential to promote a new approach to environmental and social safeguards. The project will encourage an enabling environment and catalyse other projects to adopt similar approaches. This will lead to a paradigm shift in how environmental and social safeguard policies and goals are implemented, particularly in the context of sustainable development management practices which is currently underdeveloped in Mauritania. There is also potential for the project to enhance the capacity of local institutions to critically engage stakeholders in policy formulation.

### Sustainable development potential

We acknowledge IAP’s assessment that the project’s sustainable development potential is “low” owing to IAP’s concerns about the project’s long-term sustainability and its limited scope. We note that the recommended IAP modifications will address these issues.

## Part 1: Gender Analysis

### 1.1. Introduction
Gender analysis is the process of examining the relationship between gender and development, and the implications for policy in order to promote equality and equity. It involves identifying the ways in which gender affects people's lives and their opportunities, as well as the impact of policies on different groups. Gender analysis helps to identify the root causes of inequality and to develop strategies to address them.

Gender analysis is a critical tool for promoting gender equality and empowering women and marginalized groups. It enables policymakers to understand the barriers that prevent women from participating fully in society, and to develop policies that are responsive to their needs. Gender analysis also helps to identify the ways in which policies can be designed to promote gender equality and to challenge stereotypes.

### 1.2. Methodology
The methodology used to conduct this gender analysis includes qualitative data collection methods, including interviews with stakeholders and partners. Gender Action Plan indicators were used as a basis for the analysis. The data was collected from various sources, including government documents, non-governmental organizations, and community-based organizations.

The data was analyzed using a gender lens to identify the barriers that prevent women from participating fully in society. The analysis also identified the ways in which policies can be designed to promote gender equality and to challenge stereotypes.

### 1.3. Women's Rights in Montserrat
Montserrat is a signatory to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) and has ratified several other international conventions that protect women's rights, including the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights, the Convention on the Rights of the Child, and the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination.

In 2019, Montserrat ratified the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (Maputo Protocol). The Maputo Protocol is a comprehensive document that addresses the rights of women in Africa. It includes provisions on equality before the law, the right to education, the right to health care, and the right to participate in political and economic decision-making processes.

The Maputo Protocol also includes provisions on the elimination of discrimination against women in employment, the right to equal pay for equal work, and the right to protection from violence. The Protocol also provides for the establishment of a mechanism to monitor the implementation of the Protocol and to ensure that the rights of women are protected.

Montserrat is a member of the United Nations, the Commonwealth, and the Organization of Eastern Caribbean States. Montserrat has ratified several international conventions that protect women's rights, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) and the Convention on the Rights of the Child.

### 1.4 General conditions of women households

According to the National Human Rights Commission, the incidence of violence against women in Bangladesh is estimated to be around 50%. The Gender Inequality Index for Bangladesh was 0.632 in 2019. In 2018, the Gender Development Index was 0.748.

The legal age for marriage is 18 for both women and men (Personal Status Code, Art. 1), with child marriage being illegal. However, the prevalence of early marriage is high. Despite having the goal to eliminate child marriage by 2021, the incidence of child marriage has increased in recent years. According to UNICEF, around 59% of women aged between 20 and 24 were married before they turned 18. In 2017, 29% of girls were married before the age of 15.

Under the Personal Status Code, marriage is defined as a union between a man and a woman and requires the consent of both parties. Polygamy is prohibited by law. According to the law, legal marriage comprises the bride giving something (mahr) to the groom in exchange for his promise to support her financially. The husband has the right to divorce his wife without any reason. Divorce does not require a court order; a man can simply pronounce "talaq" three times and the divorce is effective immediately. Civil courts can issue orders of maintenance or alimony, but these are rarely enforced.

According to the National Human Rights Commission, 87% of women in Bangladesh have experienced some form of violence. Domestic violence is widespread and often goes unreported. According to a survey by the Bangladesh Bureau of Statistics, around 50% of married women reported experiencing physical or sexual violence from their husbands. The most common forms of violence were physical (49%), psychological (36%), and sexual (18%).

Although women and their bodies have undergone rights, women's subordination has continued from one generation to another. In some countries, when a woman marries, her family takes over her property, and she has no right to own or inherit anything. ## Rural women play an important role in agricultural production

Rural women play an important role in agricultural production. Although only 25.4% of women in the country are employed, they contribute significantly to food security and household income. They are responsible for all tasks related to food production and household management. They are also involved in processing and marketing of products. Women play an important role in the development of rural communities by providing services such as childcare, health care, and education. They are also responsible for maintaining the home and caring for family members.

### Import of Climate Change

At a wider level, Malawi's National Adaptation Programme of Action to Climate Change (2008) and the National Adaptation Strategy and Plan of Action (NASPA) have been developed to address climate change. These strategies aim to reduce vulnerability to climate change by improving adaptive capacity and resilience. For rural women, climate change has a significant impact on their livelihoods. They are particularly vulnerable to changes in weather patterns, which affect agricultural productivity. Climate change also affects the availability of water resources, which is critical for agriculture and livestock production. Women are responsible for collecting water and firewood, and they are often the first to suffer from the effects of climate change.

The West African Sahel has experienced enormous changes in land use due to climate change that was exacerbated by human activities. The region is characterized by low agricultural productivity and high vulnerability to drought and other natural disasters. The impacts of climate change have been exacerbated by poor governance, weak institutions, lack of infrastructure, inadequate access to credit, and limited capacity for adaptation. These factors have led to increased food insecurity, water scarcity, and soil degradation. The region has also experienced significant changes in land use patterns, including deforestation, bush encroachment, and desertification. This has led to a decrease in agricultural productivity and an increase in the number of people living in poverty.

#### 1.7.1 Land Rights Act

The Land Rights Act was passed in Mountains in 2013. The act aims to increase access to land for women and the poor. The act provides for equal rights to land for men and women, and it also provides for the recognition of customary land rights. However, the act has not been fully implemented and there is still a significant gap between the rights of men and women.

## 7.8 Access to and control over resources

In Mauritius, considerable gender disparities exist in men's and women's access to and control over productive resources such as land, water, and credit. Deeply ingrained socio-cultural norms regulate women's rights to own property and inherit land. Women have limited access to land, water, and credit, which are essential for agricultural production.

Women's participation in decision-making processes related to valuable resources, including water management and land use, is minimal. They lack control over productive assets such as land, livestock, and equipment. Women's participation in resource management, including farming, fishing, and forestry, is limited by socio-cultural norms that restrict their mobility and access to markets.

## 7.9 Gender and agricultural households

Gender roles are deeply embedded within the agricultural sector of Mauritius. Women are often engaged in unpaid domestic work while men are involved in productive activities such as farming, livestock rearing, and fishing. Women's participation in labor-saving technologies is limited by their lack of access to credit and other resources.

Women play a crucial role in household food security and nutrition. They are responsible for preparing meals and ensuring that the family has enough to eat. Women also contribute significantly to agricultural production through their involvement in farming activities such as planting, harvesting, and processing crops.

### Participation of Women in Policy

Women’s participation in policy-making processes is crucial for addressing gender inequalities. In 2019, the government introduced several measures to ensure and increase the participation of women in the public and private sectors. These include:

- **Increased representation**: The government increased the number of women in decision-making positions.
- **Policy reforms**: Reforms were implemented to promote gender equality.

By 2021, only 25% of seats in parliament were held by women, indicating a slow pace of change.

### Women in Leadership

Women in leadership roles are essential for promoting gender equality. In 2019, the government created an active plan to advance gender equality and promote women’s rights. This plan included:

- **Training programs**: Training programs were established to empower women.
- **Policy reforms**: Reforms were implemented to address gender inequalities.

Despite these measures, women still face significant barriers in public service, particularly in management positions. The government has committed to addressing these issues through various initiatives and policies.

### Access to Justice

The justice system in Malawi concerns of issues that affect the principles of both men and women equally. However, women often face unique challenges due to cultural norms and societal expectations. For example:

- **Legal aid**: Legal aid is not always available.
- **Cultural barriers**: Cultural barriers prevent women from accessing justice.

Women’s access to justice is a critical issue in promoting gender equality. According to the 2018 World Bank report, only 35% of women have access to legal aid compared to 45% of men.

# Gender-based violence (GBV)

Although there are no official less prominent domestic violence in Mauritania, the government has taken steps to address the issue. In 2015, the government established a national committee on women's issues and appointed a minister for women's affairs. The government also provides assistance to victims of domestic violence through various programs.

Domestic violence is a pervasive problem throughout the country. Under the Penal Code, rape is a criminal offense punishable by up to five years in prison and a fine of up to 100,000 ouguiya ($285). However, the law does not specifically address domestic violence, and police rarely intervene in cases of domestic abuse. Domestic violence is often considered a private family matter rather than a criminal offense.

To date, there has been no significant media coverage or awareness campaigns on domestic violence. The government has not established any shelters for victims of domestic violence. There are no known nongovernmental organizations (NGOs) that provide support to victims of domestic violence.

### Gender

#### Women's Issues

- Women's access to land ownership
  - Limited access to land ownership
  - Limited participation in decision-making processes
  - Limited access to education and training opportunities

- Women's rights awareness and advocacy
  - Training on leadership and governance
  - Access to vocational training and education

#### Men's Issues

- Men's role in gender equality initiatives
  - Limited involvement in gender equality initiatives
  - Limited understanding of gender issues
  - Limited participation in household decision-making

- Workshops on gender equality and women's empowerment
  - Community dialogues and awareness campaigns
  - Training on shared decision-making and gender roles

### Solutions

#### Women's Issues

- Women's land rights awareness and advocacy
  - Training on leadership and governance
  - Access to vocational training and education

#### Men's Issues

- Workshops on gender equality and women's empowerment
  - Community dialogues and awareness campaigns
  - Training on shared decision-making and gender roles

Gender change when men and women differ, with cultural practices making some more vulnerable than men. Mainstreaming gender into the strengthening of the resilience of ecosystems and livelihoods is essential for sustainable development.


---
**Page 115**
---

It is therefore essential to build the adaptive capacity of households and communities in the four hills of northern Myanmar. The results from this general assessment will guide the design of project activities that aim to enhance the resilience of these communities. This approach will be based on the understanding learned from the cultural needs of men, women, and children. The proposed project will focus on enhancing the adaptive capacity of households and communities in the four hills through:

- **Strengthening local governance** to improve the delivery of services at the village level (Innpaing).
- **Enhancing women's participation** in all levels of decision-making processes.
- **Promoting gender equality** by addressing the root causes of gender inequality, including unequal access to resources and opportunities for women.
- **Supporting local adaptation initiatives**, with a particular focus on climate resilient agriculture and livelihoods.
- **Improving the capacity of local governments** to support the needs of vulnerable groups in their communities.

### 2.3.2 Gender mainstreaming in the project design

Gender mainstreaming ensures visibility and support for the purposes and benefits of both women and men, and promotes gender equality and equity in all aspects of the project. This will be achieved through:

- **Gender analysis** to identify the different needs and constraints faced by women and men.
- **Participatory approaches**, including gender-sensitive planning and budgeting processes.

It is also essential to build capacity within a greater base of local leaders, especially women, to ensure that the benefits from project activities are sustained. This will be achieved through:

- **Training** in gender equality, climate change adaptation, and disaster risk reduction.
- **Support for local organizations**, including women's groups, to enhance their dynamic capacities and improve their ability to engage in community development processes.

The gender assessment and related analysis inform the goals of project activities and ensure that they are gender-sensitive. The project will also support the establishment of a gender focal point (GFP) within the project team to promote gender equality and equity throughout the project cycle, including planning, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation. This GFP will be responsible for ensuring that all aspects of the project are gender-sensitive.

### 2.3.1. Gender equality in agricultural development

Gender equality is a pre-requisite for sustainable agricultural development. Women play an important role in agriculture and rural development. They contribute to food security by producing food crops, rearing livestock, processing agricultural produce and marketing the surplus. However, women are often denied access to land, credit, extension services and other inputs necessary for increasing their productivity. This is due to cultural and socio-economic factors that limit their ability to participate fully in decision-making processes.

### 2.3.2. Women's participation in agricultural development

Women's participation in agricultural development is essential for achieving food security and sustainable agriculture. Women are involved in all aspects of agricultural production, from planting to harvesting and marketing. However, they often face discrimination and barriers to their full participation in decision-making processes. These barriers include lack of access to land, credit, education and training, as well as cultural attitudes that limit their ability to participate fully in the economy.

# Gender Equality in Organizations (GEOs)

## A. Ensuring gender equality and women’s empowerment

### a. Ensure that organizations have policies and procedures to address gender equality and women’s empowerment issues.

### b. Ensure that organizations have mechanisms for monitoring and evaluating their performance on gender equality and women’s empowerment issues.

### c. Ensure that organizations have systems in place to track and report on progress towards gender equality and women’s empowerment goals.

## B. Promoting women’s leadership and participation

### a. Promote the equal representation of women and men at all levels of decision-making within the organization, including senior management positions.

### b. Implement policies and practices that support the career advancement of women, such as mentoring programs, flexible work arrangements, and family-friendly policies.

## C. Addressing gender-based violence

### a. Develop and implement policies and procedures to prevent and address gender-based violence in the workplace.

### b. Provide training and support to employees who have experienced or witnessed gender-based violence.

### c. Establish partnerships with external organizations to provide additional resources and support for addressing gender-based violence.

## D. Ensuring equal pay

### a. Ensure that women and men receive equal pay for equal work.

### b. Implement policies and practices to prevent discrimination in compensation based on gender.

# PAEs

- Promote gender equality, non-discrimination, and diversity.
- Address gender-based violence and harassment.
- Integrate gender perspectives into all aspects of the organization’s work.
- Ensure that women’s leadership is promoted and supported.
- Monitor and report on progress towards gender equality goals.

# Tasks 1-2

- Review existing policies and procedures related to gender equality and women’s empowerment.
- Identify areas for improvement and develop action plans to address them.

# Strategic Planning

## Objectives

- **Objective 1:** To expand our market presence and increase sales by 20% within the next fiscal year.
- **Objective 2:** To enhance brand awareness through targeted marketing campaigns.

### Target Market

- **Target:** All users who prefer innovative, high-quality products.
- **Demographics:** Age range of 18 to 45 years old.
- **Psychographics:** Tech-savvy individuals interested in emerging digital technologies and innovations.

## Marketing Strategies

### Branding

- **Strategy:** Develop a strong brand identity that resonates with our target audience.
- **Tactics:**
  - Create consistent branding across all marketing channels.
  - Utilize social media to engage with customers and build a community around the brand.
  - Implement digital advertising campaigns to increase visibility.

### Product Development

- **Strategy:** Focus on developing innovative products that meet customer needs.
- **Tactics:**
  - Conduct market research to identify trends and gaps in the market.
  - Invest in R&D to create cutting-edge technology solutions.
  - Collaborate with industry experts to ensure product quality and reliability.

### Sales and Distribution

- **Strategy:** Establish a robust sales and distribution network.
- **Tactics:**
  - Partner with key retailers to increase product availability.
  - Leverage e-commerce platforms to reach a wider audience.
  - Implement effective inventory management systems to ensure timely delivery of products.

## Financial Projections

### Revenue Streams

- **Stream 1:** Sales of core products.
- **Stream 2:** Sales of accessories and complementary products.
- **Stream 3:** Licensing and partnership revenue.

### Expenses

- **Category:** Research and Development
- **Amount:** $500,000
- **Category:** Marketing and Advertising
- **Amount:** $300,000
- **Category:** Operational Costs
- **Amount:** $200,000

### Profit Margin

- **Expected Margin:** 15%

## Milestones

### Q1-Q2

- Launch initial marketing campaign.
- Secure partnerships with key retailers.

### Q3-Q4

- Release new product line.
- Expand distribution channels.

# Section 1.2

Enhance the ability of advanced learners to use appropriate grammar and vocabulary when speaking.

## Targets

### Years 3-4

1. **Vocabulary**
   - Use a wide range of vocabulary with precision.
   - Understand and use specialist vocabulary.
   - Use vocabulary to discuss their own and others' experiences, feelings and aspirations.

2. **Grammar**
   - Use a wider range of structures when speaking.
   - Use passive voice correctly.
   - Use modal verbs accurately in different tenses.

3. **Listening**
   - Listen for specific information related to particular topics within a familiar context.
   - Identify and interpret implicit meanings in spoken language.
   - Follow and understand complex spoken arguments and presented points of view.

4. **Speaking**
   - Speak confidently and effectively, varying language according to context.
   - Use appropriate register for different purposes.
   - Use spoken language to accomplish a range of goals.

### Years 5-6

1. **Vocabulary**
   - Use advanced vocabulary with precision.
   - Understand and use specialist vocabulary in different contexts.
   - Use vocabulary to discuss complex ideas, feelings and aspirations.

2. **Grammar**
   - Use a wide range of structures accurately when speaking.
   - Use passive voice correctly in different tenses.
   - Use modal verbs accurately in different contexts.

3. **Listening**
   - Listen for specific information related to particular topics within an unfamiliar context.
   - Identify and interpret implicit meanings in spoken language.
   - Follow and understand complex spoken arguments and presented points of view.

4. **Speaking**
   - Speak confidently and effectively, varying language according to context.
   - Use appropriate register for different purposes.
   - Use spoken language to accomplish a range of goals.

### Years 7-8

1. **Vocabulary**
   - Use advanced vocabulary with precision in different contexts.
   - Understand and use specialist vocabulary accurately.
   - Use vocabulary to discuss complex ideas, feelings and aspirations.

2. **Grammar**
   - Use a wide range of structures accurately when speaking.
   - Use passive voice correctly in different tenses.
   - Use modal verbs accurately in different contexts.

3. **Listening**
   - Listen for specific information related to particular topics within an unfamiliar context.
   - Identify and interpret implicit meanings in spoken language.
   - Follow and understand complex spoken arguments and presented points of view.

4. **Speaking**
   - Speak confidently and effectively, varying language according to context.
   - Use appropriate register for different purposes.
   - Use spoken language to accomplish a range of goals.

# Summary of their needs

## M: Engage people with disabilities in community development and humanitarian action to ensure that they are included in all aspects of society.

- Ensure that people with disabilities have access to services and opportunities for participation.
- Promote the inclusion of children with disabilities in schools, families, and communities.
- Advocate for the rights of people with disabilities and promote their full participation in society.

## S: Strengthen local police capacity and accountability to ensure respect for human rights and the rule of law.

- Provide training on human rights and international humanitarian law to police officers.
- Establish independent oversight mechanisms to monitor police performance and investigate allegations of abuse.
- Promote community policing initiatives that involve local communities in the maintenance of public order.

## P: Promote peaceful coexistence among different ethnic, religious, and political groups.

- Support initiatives that foster dialogue and understanding between different groups.
- Encourage community-based conflict resolution mechanisms.
- Promote the inclusion of marginalized groups in decision-making processes.

# Actions to be taken by relevant actors

## Activity 1: Strengthen local police capacity

### M: Provide training on human rights and international humanitarian law for police officers.

- Conduct workshops and seminars for police officers on human rights and international humanitarian law.
- Develop training materials that are culturally sensitive and tailored to the needs of police officers in different regions.

### S: Establish independent oversight mechanisms for the police.

- Create an independent commission to investigate allegations of police abuse.
- Ensure that the commission has the resources and authority to conduct effective investigations.
- Promote transparency and accountability within the police force by making reports on its findings public.

### P: Support initiatives that promote peaceful coexistence among different groups.

- Facilitate dialogue between different ethnic, religious, and political groups.
- Provide support for community-based conflict resolution mechanisms.
- Encourage the participation of marginalized groups in decision-making processes.

## Activity 2: Promote the inclusion of people with disabilities

### M: Ensure that people with disabilities have access to services and opportunities for participation.

- Develop policies and programs that promote the inclusion of people with disabilities in all aspects of society.
- Provide support for organizations that advocate for the rights of people with disabilities.
- Promote the use of assistive technologies to enhance the participation of people with disabilities.

### S: Strengthen local police capacity to protect the rights of people with disabilities.

- Provide training on the rights of people with disabilities for police officers.
- Establish mechanisms for reporting and addressing cases of abuse against people with disabilities.
- Promote the inclusion of people with disabilities in community development initiatives.

### P: Support initiatives that promote peaceful coexistence among different groups.

- Facilitate dialogue between different ethnic, religious, and political groups.
- Provide support for community-based conflict resolution mechanisms.
- Encourage the participation of marginalized groups in decision-making processes.

# Targets

## M: 100% of new police officers receive training on human rights and international humanitarian law.

## S: 50% of new police officers are trained by independent oversight mechanisms.

## P: 75% of communities have access to conflict resolution mechanisms.

# Indicators

## M: The number of people with disabilities participating in community development initiatives increases by 20%.

## S: The number of cases investigated by independent oversight mechanisms increases by 15%.

## P: The number of communities with access to conflict resolution mechanisms increases by 30%.

# Regional Priorities

## M: Regional Police Coordination

- Strengthen the capacity of police forces in different regions to work together.
- Promote the exchange of best practices and experiences among police forces.

## S: Regional Police Coordination

- Strengthen the capacity of police forces in different regions to work together.
- Promote the exchange of best practices and experiences among police forces.

## P: Regional Priorities

- Support initiatives that promote peaceful coexistence among different groups.
- Encourage community-based conflict resolution mechanisms.

# The cost of implementing the strategy

- The cost of implementing the strategy is estimated at $5 million per year.
